851. EDWARD PATTERSON was indicted for stealing, as the 12th of February, 1 saw, value 5 s.; 2 planes, value 6 s.; the goods of John Perry: and that he had been before convicted of felony.
JOHN PERRY . I keep the Ship and Sailor ale-house, in Church-street, Greenwich. I lost several tools—I had left home for a fortnight in February, and in my absence they were taken—I had used them the day before I left—the prisoner has frequently used my tap-room—I did not authorise him to pawn any of these articles.
EDWARD BOLTON . I am an apprentice to Mr. Harber, a pawnbroker, at Greenwhich. On the 12th of February a saw and plane were pawned at master's by the prisoner—I recollect him—1 s. 6 d. was advanced for the two—I have the ticket.
CHARLES WILLIAMS . I am a pawnbroker at Greenwhich. On Saturday evening, the prisoner came with this plane, which he wanted 4 s. on—I asked him whose it was—he said his father's—I said I should like to see his father; and on the Monday he came and said, "Mr. Williams, my father says he is unable to come, and unless you advance the money or restore the plane, he shall seek further redress"—I was taken off my guard, and lent him the 2 s. THOMAS LARKIN. I apprehended the prisoner at his mother's house, in Greenwhich, last Thursday—I told him the charge—he denied it, and said he knew nothing about the tools.
( Property produced sworn to.) JAMES WILD. I am a policeman. I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former condviction, which I got from Mr. Straight's office—I was present at Maidstone when the prisoner was tried and convicted there in March, 1834 ( read). GUILTY . Aged 19— Transported for Seven Years.

852. SARAH DABIN was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of February, 4 bolsters, value 1 l.; 10 s.; 3 Pillows, value 1 l.; 3 blankets, value 15 s.; 4 counterpanes, value 16 s.; 5 1/2 yards of carpet. value 10 s.; 1 looking glass and stand, 5 s.; 1 set of fire-irons, value 5 s.; 1 tea-caddy, value 7 s.; 1 sugar-basin, value 3 s.; 1 cork-screw, value 5 s.; 1 hearth-brush, value 1 s.; 4 yards of drugget, value 10 s.; and 5 candlestricks, value 2 s.; the goods of James Lester, her master.
JAMES LESTER . I am an attorney, living at the Grove, Blackheath. The prisoner was employed by me from some time in October till the 6th of February—I have missed bolsters, pillows, blankets, a tea-caddy, sugar-bais and other things—this tea-caddy is mine, and some of these other things—the prisoner had on opportunity of getting access to these things—the looking-glass is mine.
WILLIAM SPENCER . I am a pawnbroker, and live in Leonard-street, Greenwhich. I have produced the tea-caddy—I received it on the 6th of February from the prisoner.
JOHN ABETHELL . I am a pawnbroker, and live on Blackheath-hill. This looking-glass was pledged on the 10th of February by the prisoner—this candlestick was pledged by her also.
HANNAH WARREN . I know all the property as being Mr. Lester's—I lived in his house five months.
Prisoner. I was in distress, and afraid of being ill-used by my husband—I had no intention of stealing them—I had one of my children ill for ten months.
GUILTY . Aged 45.— Confined Six Months.

853. ANN KEENAN was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling—house of William Stewart, on the 11th of February, and stealing therein 1 clock, value 20 s., his goods,
CHARLOTTE STEWART . I am the wife of William Stewart, and live in Bell—court, Woolwich. On the 11th of February I went out about ten o'clock, and returned about twelve—I left my own boy at home—he is twelve years old—I occupy two rooms on one side of the passage—the landlord lives in the next house to us—I left the room open which my boy was in, and my cloak was in the room—when I came back my cloak was gone—the prisoner lived in a room in the same passage.
EDWARD WOOLWICH , I have o'clock pawned by the prisoner, I believe—in the name of Ann Scott—I did not swear to the prisoner positively when I was before the Magistrate—I said, to the best of my belief the prisoner was the person.
GEORGE WHITEHEAD . My mother left me at home, but I did not stay—I do not know who took the cloak—when I went out, I locked the door and left the key in it.
Prisoner's Defence. I hope you will have mercy on me and my child. I was in distress, and want led me to do it. NOT GUILTY

854. JOSEPH SHARP was indicted for feloniously assaulting Jerome McGurrin, on the 23rd of October, With intent to rob him, and his money goods, and chattels from his person and against his will, feloniously and violently to steal, take, and carry away.
JEROME MCGURRIN . On the 23rd of September I took a house from Henry Clare, and bought the goods 5 l.—the prisoner pretended to be a broker, to let the house for Norris, the old landlord—on the 20th of October he put an execution into the house, and on the 23rd he took away the goods—after sending away the goods, he knocked me down, to get away the warrant from me—he represented himself to be the freeholder of the certain—I had a £5 note and some halfpence in my pocket—he said he wanted the warrant—I would not give it to him, and he knocked me dowa—the warrant shows he was not the freeholder of the house—it was a false execution that he put in—I have been tried for robbing this house, and was honourably acquitted—he put in an execution and took my goods away twice—he was in the habit of doing this, and has been convicted twice in this Court for taking goods by false distresses—I took the house at 20 l. a year—when he struck me he said, "What is the use of talking to me? I told the inspector of the police I was the freeholder, but I am nothing but an agent to him"—I kept the warrant from him—he knocked me down and ransacked my pocket; and my wife went for the policemen, but they would not ender into it—I went to Union Hall—they said I must go to a higher court—I was robbed twice by him, and my goods sold—here is the warrant—he did not say any thing about the money I had in my pocket—he said he wanted the warrant back—he collared me, he and Clare, and knocked me down to take it out of my breeches' pocket, but I kept it—he took nothing out—he put his hand to the pocket the note was in.
Cross-examined by MR. DUNBAR. Q. Did you find the £5 note safe in your pocket? A. I did; it was a £I did not put the warrant in my pocket, but into my boot—I kept the warrant as a proof he was net the freeholder or broker—he knocked me down to rob me of my money.
JOHN BLAKE . On the 23rd of October I went down to Mr. M'Gunin for the carriage—hire for moving goods for him—I saw Clare and Sharp wrestling with him—they had got him down, and one of them put his hand into his pocket and tried to take something out, and he tried to prevent them from taking it out—they did not say what they wanted to get, in my presence.
Cross-exaimned. Q. This was in a public—house, was it not? A. Yes; it was between four and five o'clock in the afternoon—in a public room—not a likely place to rob a man—no mention was made of the warrant, in my presence.
NOT GUILTY

856. ROBERT WINTER was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of February, 1 handkerchief, the goods of Ethelburt Turner, from his person.
ETHELBERT TURNER . On the 15th of February, about twelve o'clock at night, I was in Joiner—street, Borough—a gentleman call out, "You are robbed"—I turned round and saw three person cress over—I seized the middle one—he said, "That is not the one, it is the other"—I then seized the prisoner, and accused him of stealing my handkerchief—he said he had not got it—but he put his hand in his pocket and drew it out immediately—this is it—it is mine—the prisoner was close to me when he was pointed out—I had not felt it taken.
EDWARD WOOD . I am a green—grocer. I was on the opposite side of the road—the prisoner and two other were walking together in company—the prosecutor was on before them them—I saw the prosecutor's coat—tail lifted up, and his handkerchief being drawn from his pocket—I could not tell for a moment whether it was the prisoner or a companion, but I saw the prisoner withdraw his hand with the handkerchief in it, and put it into his pocket—I called to the prosecutor, and pointed the prisoner out—he denied having the handkerchief—I said he had got it—he then put his hand into his breeches pocket and gave it in to the prosecutor.
GUILTY. Aged 16.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18360229-857]
857. WILLIAM OVER was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of February, 1 purse, value 1 s.; 1 double sovereign; 5 sovereigns; and 7 half-sovereigns; the goods and monies of Nathan Delph, his master.
NATHAN DELPH . I am a surgeon, and live in Alfred—place, St. George's, Southwark. The prisoner was my errand—boy for about six months—I sent him with some trowsers, to be cleaned, on the 11th of February, about half-past nine—o'clock in the morning, to Mr. Rolls, the dyer—I saw him again in five minutes; and about four o'clock in the afternoon I missed my purse,
containing a double-sovereign, five sovereigns, and seven half-sovereigns—it was a common leather purse—I recollected leaving it in the fob-pocket of the trowsers I had sent to the dyer's, having worn them the day before—I directly went to Rolls, and saw the trowsers—the purse was not there—I told a young woman there the contents of the purse—the prisoner went out with me in my chaise, and after he had taken out the medicine in the afternoon, I said to him, "You took the trowsers to the dyer's, was there any thing in the pocket?"—"Yes," he said, "there was a lump, Sir, which I took with the trowsers, and laid them on the counter, at Mr. Rolls"—that he carried the lump in his hand, and laid it on the counter, and told the young woman to get the trowsers done as soon as possible.
Prisoner. I left the lump with the trowsers—I did not know it was a purse as I was going along the street I felt a lump, but took no notice of it—I left the trowsers at the dyer's, and returned to my master directly; and in the afternoon he said, "You took my trowsers, did you feel any thing in them?"—I said, "Yes, a lump, but I did not take any notice of it—and when I got to the shop I left it there"—he asked if I could take my oath that I left the lump—I said, "Yes"—he said, "Well, go to the dyer's and ask for the trowsers, and examine them," which I did—I said to the young woman, "When I brought them, there was a lump in them"—she said, "No, there was not, "—but she said there were five sovereigns, a double-sovereign, and seven half-sovereigns—I came back to master, and he said, "How the devil should she know what was in the purse?"—he said, "Put your hat on"—I went back with him—he said, "Fetch up your box," which I did, and he searched it, but found nothing—he said, "Go to Rolls, and say I will have a search-warrant"—I went there, and they said, "The sooner the better"—master asked if I could take my oath that I left the lump there, and I said I could.
NATHAN DELPH re-examined. It was a slate-coloured leather purse, with two slides—the five sovereigns were at one end—I had told the contents before the prisoner went back.
HARRIETT MAY . I have been three years at Mr. Rolls' next August—I saw the prisoner when he brought the trowsers, about a quarter to ten o'clock on the 11th of February—he said he had brought his master's trowsers to be cleaned, and asked when they would be done—I said on Saturday; and as soon as they were done we would send them home—he left them—I laid them down behind the counter till a quarter to twelve o'clock, when I entered them in the day-book—I did not feel any thing in the pocket—if there had been the smallest thing possible in the fob I must have felt it—I am positive there was no lump at all—the prisoner said nothing to me about a lump—the trowsers were rolled up when he brought them; but I opened them when I entered them in the day-book—I threw them open on the counter—the flap came completely open—I saw all the pockets and the fob, and am certain there was nothing in them.
Prisoner Q. You did not open them till twelve o'clock? A. Not till a quarter to twelve o'clock—there was nobody in the shop but me—the workmen do not go through that part of the shop—I never stirred from the counter from the time they were brought—there was a young woman, but she never went near the trowsers—her back was towards me—she never touches any thing—she sat there till one o'clock.
BENJAMIN ELMES . I am turnkey of Surrey gaol. On the 11th of February I received the prisoner in charge—seeing him in livery as a servant, I asked what he was charged with—he said with stealing five sovereigns—I said,
"Well, you know better about it than I do: how is it?"—he said, "Master ordered me to take a pair of trowsers to be scoured, and in going along a lump dropped out of the pocket on the ground—I picked it up"—I said, "What did you do with it?"—he said, "I took it with the trowsers, one in my right hand, and the other in my left, to Mr. Rolls"—I said, "Why, you did not leave the purse there, did you?"—he said, "Yes, I did: I thought it all belonged to the trowsers." Prisoner. I said, "I felt a lump:" not that I saw what it was, nor that I picked it up. Witness. He did: I am certain.
NATHAN DELPH re-examined. He told me that he laid the lump on the counter with the trowsers—that he carried the lump in his left hand, and wrapped the trowsers over it, and laid it on the counter.
Prisoner's Defence. If I had felt the lump I might have supposed it contained money—the lump did not fall from the trowsers—it was with the trowsers when I laid them down.
NATHAN DELPH re-examined. He has a father and mother—the policeman searched him and his box, but nothing was found—he told me he turned the trowsers over the lump, and laid them down all together.
NOT GUILTY
Second Jury, before Mr. Recorder.

858. MARY FOLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of February, 17 glass phials, value 1 s.; and 3 lbs. of candles, value 8 1/2 d.; the goods of John Geevan, her master.
JAMES GEEVAN . I am apprenticed to my brother, John Geevan, and live in the house. The prisoner was his housekeeper up to the 11th of February from the 24th of November—I marked some candles, having suspicion, and sent them down into the kitchen, for the use of the house—I called in a constable on the 11th of February, who searched her box in her presence—it was not locked—he took out six mould candles, and one kitchen candle—four of the mould candles were what I had marked—he them went to a chest of drawers in her bed-room, and found eleven glass bottles, rolled up a separately in paper—the drawer was not locked—she said she took them to make ornaments, and produced some bottles which she had made some kind of ornaments of.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. This was on the 11th of February? A. Yes; she went down on her knees and begged pardon, and left our service that day—we had then found all the articles in the indictment, but afterwards missed linen—she attended at dinner that day, after the constable had been, and afterwards left the house, promising to bring some things back on the next day, which she did not—a warrant was granted on the 17th of February—she brought a table-cloth and shirt back when she was sent after them on the 14th of February—her wages were due from the time she came—I believe my brother went to her cousin's house—I was not present if he said any thing about her wages—I saw Haynes her cousin come there—my brother is not here—the phials were wrapped up in separate papers, and put in a bag—she said they were her master's, and that she took them to make ornaments—I said "You have taken them out of the surgery"—she said, "No"—I said, "Well, it was from down stairs"—she was never allowed to burn mould candles in kitchen—it was the duty of the errand-boy to fetch things for the house—the candles and every thing else are under his charge—she said the candles were her master's, and she had taken them to give to a sister who was out of a situation she was apprehended on the 19th of February.
COURT. Q. What did her wages amount to? A. Fourteen guinens a-year—she was not taken before, as my brother wished in consult his lawyer, who told him he would be put to 20 l. expense.
JOSEPH POWELL ( police-constable L 2). I went to the prosecutors's house, and searched the prisoner's box—I found six mould candles, a kitchen candle, and the phials she said the property belonged to her master, and burst out crying. Mr. Geevan said he would not prosecute her and I went away—she did not tell me why she took the candles.
Prisoner. I took them for the use of the house—I got up early in the morning and told the boy to bring a pound of kitchen candles, and he brought moulds instead: I said I would take them away, and put them in my box, as I should have them when I was short.
NOT GUILTY
Third Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

859. GEORGE ATTERTON was indicted for stealing on the 3rd of December, 1 glazier's diamond, value 30 s., the goods of William Walker; and WILLIAM JOHNSON for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to be stolen, against the Statute, &c.
WILLIAM WALKER . I am a painter and glazier, and live in Gravel-lane. The prisoner Atterton is the son of a gardener, employed at Mr. Druces's, Denmark-hill, Camberwell—I was at work there and the other prisoner came backwards and forwards—I saw him there—I missed the diamond on the 23rd of December—it was taken from a place on the premises which I used as a shop—I had seen the prisoner on the premises coming backwards and forwards, and he had access to the property—this is it—it is worth 30 s.—I have had it six years.
SAMUEL CHAPMAN . I am a pawnbroker, and live in Lock's-fields. This diamond was pawned with me—I do not know who by.
JOHN CALLIGAN . I am a policeman. I received information on the 10th of February, from Atterton's brother and another boy, who came to me—I went with them to Johnson's and asked him what he had done with the diamond which he had bought—he asked me what diamond—I said the one he bought of Atterton—he said he never bought one at all—I said there was a boy there could prove he had bought it; and the boy said, "You did buy it: you gave him 6 d. and a pennyworth of apples for it"—I asked him where it was—he said in pledge—I asked where—he said at Mr. Chapman's—I asked him where the duplicate was—he said he had given his mother the duplicate to put away with the rest—the mother went and got it—I took him into custody—Johnson said he pawned it for 4 s.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. Did you say the boy's mother gave you the duplicate? A. No; his brother brought me the duplicate, but he got it from the mother—I do not know that she was in the habit of sending him to pawn.
NOT GUILTY

860. WILLIAM GREEN was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of February, 1 cape, value 5 s., the goods of John Searl.

Prisoner's Defence. I work for Mr. Harris, who comes to Billingsgate market—on the 4th of February as I was going along Fleet-street it began to rain very heavy—I went with my master to the market; I took the cape off the horse and put it over my shoulder to keep them dry—I went home with it, and next morning the policeman stopped me with it.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined Three Months.

862. SAMUEL STEED was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
FRANCES DREW . I am the wife of Thomas Drew, who sells ladies shoes in Newington-cause way. I know the prisoner—I have seen him come at different times to purchase shoes for his master—he never paid more than 4 s. 6 d. for satin shoes—I do not recollect selling him kid shoes myself—we charge 3 s. 6 d. for them—on the 30th of December he had four pairs and he wanted something allowed for himself—I told him I could not allow any thing—I made out the bill in the regular was—I cannot say whether he wanted it allowed on the bill, or for himself afterwards—on the 6th of January he came to our shop, and bought some satin shoes—he paid 4 s. 8 d. for them—we have a fixed price, and never deviate from that—the prosecutor called on me a few days ago with the officer to make enquiries of me, and I gave him this account.
PETER HUBURN . I am a boot and shoe-maker, and live at Denmark-hill. The prisoner was in my service some years—I had reason to complain of him, and discharged him—I afterwards found there was something wrong about satin and kid shoes—I went to Mrs. Drew, and made inquiry of her, and she gave me an account about her satin and kid shoes—I had been in the habit of sending the prisoner to Drew's and other persons, to purchase kid and satin shoes, and when he returned he told me the price—on the 6th of January I sent him for satin shoes, and on his return he said he gave 5 s. for them and bought them at Mrs. Drew's—that was what I paid him—I always gave him the money before he went.
COURT. This is not embezzlement.
NOT GUILTY

[trialAccount t18360229-864]
864. JOSEPH EVEREST and WILLIAM SHORT were indicted for stealing on the 4th of March 1 stove, value 20 s.; and 1 shovel, value 3 d.; the goods of Richard Mills, in a yacht upon the navigable river Thames.
THOMAS CROWNE . I am a shipwright. This property was on board the Maria yacht, which laid at the back of Mr. Richard Mills's premises, at
Rotherhithe—he is a shipwright—it is his own vessel this happened—about the 24th of February the yacht was on the river Thames—I am foreman to Mills—the property was in my care—I received information that the stove, and a little patent hearth and shovel, and various articles, were gone from the cabin—I missed it about the 23rd of February—a witness gave me information, and I sent Evans the Thames Police surveyor, and he produced it.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. Have you fitted the stove to the place it was taken from? A. No; there is no fitting for it—the smoke goes through a funnel—part of the funnel was taken away—it was broken.
COURT. Q. Have you any doubt it is the stove? A. Not the least; it had been broken and repaired.
JOHN GILES . I am engine driver to Mr. Lewis, at Bow. I belong to the ballast engine—this stove came up in Mr. Mackintosh's barge, the Richard —my steamer laid about two miles from the yacht—they brought the barge up; and they asked my leave to put the stove on board till the barge came back from the barge builders—that was about the 24th of February.
Q. Who do you mean by they? A. Bill Short; and George Everest—they brought the barge up in the morning—it was day-light when they took the stove out—they brought the barge alongside me—they have been in the habit of bringing barges alongside for two years—I cannot tell whether it was morning, but it was day-light—they put it on board—I have asked them several times since to take it away—I give any of the lightermen leave to put things on board—Evans took the same stove from my barge.
Cross-examined. Q. It was broad day-light? A. Yes; there was no concealment—I knew them well before—the barge belongs to Mr. Fuller—they did not mention his name—I know it belonged to him.
HENRY NASH . I was sleeping forward on board the Maria, and was awoke by a great noise—I thought it was the police-boat coming on board, and I was afraid to get up, but it was Joseph Everest—he had come in a boat, and somebody else was in the boat—I knew Joe only by his voice—I said, "Who is that?"—he said, "Bring a lantern"—I said, "It is you, Joe, you have come to frighten me"—I laid hold of his tarpauling hat, and said, "Joe, is it you? you have come to frighten me?—he jumped out of a sailing boat—I did not see him take any thing; but next morning the stove was missed from the cabin—it was safe overnight.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. Are you what they call a mud-lark? A. No; I sometimes pick up things in the mud, when I can get nothing else to do—I do not not pick up coals—I pick up iron, bones, and rope—I was afraid the Thames police were coming to take me away to the Thames police-boat—I have never been there; I was sleeping on board that night, because I had no where else to sleep—I knew Joe by his voice—he said, "Bring the lantern"—I took hold of his hat: I then knew him—I knew him by his voice before—the foreman knew I slept on board this boat—I have slept there two or three months—I knew the stove was there, because I had seen it in the day-time—the cabin doors were open in the day-time—it was about ten o'clock when I went to sleep—I did not see it at night—the foreman missed it next running, or about the middle of the day—I had gone away at day-light—it had not been missed then—I did not know it was gone, when I went away in the morning—I saw the man step out of the boat into a skiff—I did not see any one with him.
JAMES CHRISTOPHER EVANS . I am a Thames police officer. I went on board the steam engine barge, and Giles gave me the stove—he told me how he got it—I apprehended short, and asked him how the stove came on board the Two Henrys —he said he knew nothing about it; but he had seen it on board one of Mr. Mackintosh's barges—I asked Everest how it came there, but he said he knew nothing about it—Short afterwards said he was present when it was put on board the Two Henrys —that it was Everest put it on board—I afterwards took Everest, and told him it was for a stove stolen from the Maria —he said he knew nothing about the stove—he had not seen it—I took him to Giles, at the Two Henrys —he said he was the man who brought it—I said, "What do you say to that?"—he said, "I shall say nothing about it?—I said, "Where did you get the stove from?"—he said, "I shall not tell you where I got it from." ( Property produced and sworn to.)
JOHN SHORT . I am uncle of one of the prisoners. He works on the river, and has charge of a barge belonging to Mr. Fuller, of Limehouse—in February last that barge wanted repairing, and it was to go to Lime-house-reach, to Mr. Tulley's yard, to be repaired—it was to pass the Gravel Engine to get there—I had seen a stove on board that barge for three weeks before the barge went to be repaired—it was not concealed at all—the Gravel Engine is a place where people constantly deposit things—it was not necessary to deposit the stove on board the barge, while it is repaired—it was taken out and left on board the engine, but I do not know who by.
NOT GUILTY
[trialAccount t18360229-865]
865. THOMAS WOOLMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of February, 4 hats, value 8 s., the goods of Alfred Simpson.
JOHN FARRER EVANS . I live with Alfred Simpson, a hat manufacturer, in Great Guildford-street, Borough. On Saturday evening, the 27th of February, about seven o'clock, I was making up the accounts of the men's work, and took it into the parlour to Mr. Simpson—during this time the prisoner was missing—he was a workman in the shop—I came back and paid the men—I called him three times, but he did not appear—I paid another man for him—he is a finisher off hats—all the men were paid off, except him—four hats were missing out of the warehouse, between six and nine o'clock that night and the Monday morning—I paid the men between half-past seven and eight o'clock—the policeman H 44 gave us information, and produced a hat—the prisoner had access to the warehouse, as the door was open.
ROBERT THORP ( police-constable H 44). I heard a noise in the street—the prisoner was in conservation with a prostitute in Ratcliffe Highway—she said he was a thief, and what he had got he had stolen—I went over to him, and asked him where he got that hat—he said at the cook's-shop—I took him there, and the man said he had brought it there—I asked him where he bought it—he said in Postern-row, Tower-hill—this was about a quarter-past one o'clock on Sunday morning, the 28th—I took him to the station-house, detained him, and found the prosecutor.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. He was in company with a woman? A. Yes, walking along the street—I saw the hat open in his hand.
HENRY HERRING . I am an outfitter. The prisoner brought four hats to me on the Saturday evening for sale—I bought three of them—I objected to the other, being damaged—I did not know him before—I have
seen him before—I understood he worked for himself—I cannot sat that I had bought goods of him before—they are Seamen's hats I gave 1 s. 6 d. each of them—it is the price I buy them at the maker—I afterwards sell them for 2 s.
Cross-examined. Q. You did not know much about him before? A. No, I have purchased of a man named Walker—I believe he was taken in charge with the prisoner.
JOHN FARRER re-examined. He went out without having his wages paid—he returned, and received 5 s. 8 d. at five minutes before eight o'clock the same day—we did not miss the hats till Monday morning—I have always given the same account of this.
(James Dodd, corn-dealer; Thomas Balustrade, Cannon-street, carmen: Henry Roslin, corn-dealer;—Wolston, Tower-hill; and William Wilson, attorney, Tower-hill-row, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY .— Confined Three Months.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

867. CHARLES MORGAN was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of February, 64 lbs. weight of lead, value 10 s., the goods of Hugh Mackintosh.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN PIERCY ( police-constable R 32.) On Friday evening, the 9th of February, I was on duty in the Lower-road, Deptford, between seven and eight o'clock—I saw the prisoner come along with another person—I heard the prisoner say he had been at work on the rail-road that day; and they had given him that for his day's work—that was a large piece of lead which he was carrying—I followed him nearly half a mile (he was in Rotherhithe when I began to follow him)—he then threw down this piece of lead and ran away—I followed him, and took him without losing sight of him—this is the lead—it weights 64lbs.
JOHN DAVIS . I am one of Mr. Mackintosh's carmen. He is a contractor for the Greenwich rail-road the prisoner was a labourer, and worked there—Mr. Alexander Mackintosh is foreman of the works—on the 8th of February he was lying down gas-pipes, and ordered me to go to Durand's wharf to get lead—the prisoner went with me—this lead is a part of what we brought from that wharf—I took it to High-street, Deptford, to Mr. Mackintosh's stable, as we were too late to take it to the works—I left the cart there at twenty minutes before seven o'clock—the prisoner had helped me to take the horses out of the cart—the next morning I went to take the lead, and missed part of it—this is one of the pieces—we had only 17 pieces; and only this one was small—the others were larger pieces.
JOHN MCINNIS . I am a foreman tom Mr. Hugh Mackintosh, the contractor for these works on the Greenwich rail-road—the prisoner hands been employed there nine weeks—this lead had not been given him in lies of wages.
GUILTY . Aged 37.— Confined Nine Months.

868. MARY MAIDMENT was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of January, 1 sheet, value 4 s.; the goods of Joshua Graves, her master; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
JOSHUA GRAVES . I live in Burdett-street, Walworth-common. The prisoner worked for my wife, who is a laundress, for about eight months—on
the 16th of January a sheet was missed, and then a table-cloth—we then spoke to the prisoner, and asked her where the sheet was—she said she knew nothing at all about it—she was away for three or four days—I went and took two officers to her house—she was hid in the cupboard, under the stairs—we were obliged to break the door—I asked her for the duplicates—she denied it, but she was taken; and on the Monday following she told me the sheet was in the Walworth-road, pawned at Mr. Barber's, for 2 s.—I went and found it there—this is the sheet.

Prisoner's Defence. Distress drove me to it.

[trialAccount t18360229-862a]
862. WILLIAM SKINNER was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of January, 5 l. pieces of handkerchiefs, value 80 l., the goods of Edward Reynolds, the younger.
EDWARD REYNOLDS, JUN . I am a silk printer, and live at Merton, in Surrey. On Friday morning, the 29th of January, we missed these things—they had been put in the drying-room on the 28th of January, in the evening—we lost 5 l. pieces of silk out of the drying-room, which is upstairs—Mr. Nicholson's premises are adjacent to ours—I never saw the prisoner before—I have heard him spoken of—he was in bed in his own house when I first saw him on Monday night when he was apprehended—we have quite lost the silk—there is a wall between Mr. Nicholson's premises and ours, and some wooden palings—I observed some footsteps in Mr. Nicholson's premises, leading to my parents—the right foot mark had two nails in the heel, and twelve nails on the side; and on the left foot were three nails in the heel, and twelve nails on the side; and on the tip bad—the party had got in at one window and out at another—and the first footmark I saw was the right foot under the window—they had got up on a lean-to, and got in—we could perfectly trace foot-marks up to the lean-to, and perfectly where they got out—there was the mark of only one person's feet—the shoes taken from the prisoner were the shoes he had on, on the night he was taken—I helped to take them off on the Tuesday morning—I could swear that these shoes were the shoes that made the marks—the nails tallied in all respects—the retreat which was traced in the footsteps went from the window he got out at as well—they got to the summer-house in Mr. Nicholson's garden—but they were not traced all the way.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. When was the robbery? A. On Thursday night, the 28th, or Friday morning—I took him into custody on Monday night—I do not know whether these shoes are commonly worn by labourers—the night we were robbed was very rainy—I do not know whether it rained next day—I cannot say whether it rained for three days—the marks remained a week afterwards in some places an inch deep—I lost fifty-one pieces, worth 80 l.—I have lost many more within the last six months.
WILLIAM WEST . On the morning after the robbery, I was sent for to
the factory—I examined it, and saw the place where a person had got out of window—it was two stories high—he must have been a tall person, or he could not have rested his foot—he had hung by his hand, and then fell down on his side—I saw a foot-mark, which I traced up a gravel walk, then to a summer-house, and then, I dare say, for a mile further, to the prisoner's house—it was a foot-mark as complete as possible—I then went back to the factory, and had some conversation with Mr. Nicholson, and from information I took the prisoner on Monday evening—we had been three times, but could not find him—we went again between ten and eleven o'clock—we knocked, but could get no answer—at last be hallooed out, "Who is there?"—we said, "Open the door, "but he would not—he laid still—we knocked again—he did not answer—he then said, "Who is there?"—Mr. Reynolds said in a feigned voice, " Jack in the Green" —he then jumped up and opened the door—we took him to the watch-house that night—he had put his shoes on, and the next morning I took the shoes and traced them along to fifty places in the marks—they tallied in all respects—in the summer-house where they had rested, there appeared some shreddings of the silk and shavings—I went into the room he had taken them from, and there was the same—on the prisoner's trowsers was the dry dirt, just where he had tumbled—these are the shoes—the left shoe has three nails in the heel, and the right two—I counted all the nails, and I found them tally—on one tip you will find a little bit deficient, and I traced that very mark to within 200 yards of the prisoner's dwelling.
Q. Did these two nails in the other heel tally exactly? A. Yes; they did—on taking the prisoner to Horsemonger-lane, Mr. Reynold's said, "I would not have cared if I had got my silk back again, as they are all new patterns"—the prisoner said, "You have not gone the right way to get them"—the prisoner's family has been in distress a long while. Cross-examined. Q. How high was the window at which the thieves got in? A. I should think fifteen or sixteen feet, and the lean too nearly reached up to the window—he got up on a gate first—he got out from another window, four or five windows off—he had got on the top of the next window below, and then jumped, I should say, ten or twelve feet—he came on his feet, and then fell on his side—I looked at the shoes while he had them on—I looked at the soles and nails—I saw the tips first—I was in the room while he was dressing that night—I told the Magistrates what he said to Mr. Reynolds about his not going the right away to work—these are shoes generally worn by labourers, but they are not all nailed the same—the prisoner lives more than a mile from the prosecutor—nearer the church—at about 100 yards from his house we get to the fields, and then there is a bye-road to Mr. Reynolds'—I know Underwood—he gave the information—it was very rainy the first part of the night, but it held up afterwards—Underwood is in Horsemonger-lane for stealing or receiving cloth.
WILLIAM WRIGHT . I am a horse-patrol. I went on the Friday morning, and took particular notice of how the party escaped from the window—I traced the foot-marks from the prosecutor's to Mr. Nicholson's Summer-house, which is close to a wall which the party got over—I took notice of the shoe-marks, which were very exact, and when I saw these shoes, I said directly, "These are the shoes that made the marks."
COURT to EDWARD REYNOLDS. Q. Is this your dwelling-house? A.
Not where the property was taken from—the prisoner said, I did not go the right way to get the property—he had no right to be on the premises.
GUILTY . Aged 47.— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18360229-870]
870. JAMES ATTREE was indicted for embezzlement.
WILLIAM PRENTICE . I lived at the Half-Moon, in the Borough. 871. ALEXANDER PAYNE was indicted for stealing for stealing, on the 27th of August, 1834, 1 pair of trowsers, value 1 l.; 1 coat, value 2 l. 1 waistcoat, value 10 s.; 4 handkerchiefs. value 1 l.; 1 pair of stockings, value 1 s.; 2 razors, value 5 s.; and 2 shirts, value 18 s.; the goods of James Shuttleworth, in the dwelling-house of Ellen Davis; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
JAMES SHUTTLEWORTH . I lodged in Market-street, St. George-the-Martyr, Southwark. The prisoner slept in the same bed with me for a mouth—I had seen these articles safe on the morning of the 27th of August, 1834, at a quarter before five o'clock—he was in bed at that time—I got up to go to work, and returned at three o'clock in the aftrernoon, and the property was all gone—there was a pair of trowers worth 1 l. a coat worth 2 l. a waistcoat worth 10 s., a pair of stockings worth 1 s., four handkerchiefs worth 1 l., tow razors worth 5 s., two shirts worth 18 s.—that is a verylow value—the prisoner never came back—I never saw him again till he was in custody—these things were all folded up, and in the cupboard—the door was not locked—I think no one could have gone there till the landlady did, at nine o'clock in the morning—he left an old coat, shirt, and waistcoat, there.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Had these things been long in wear? A. No; about three months—Mr. West made my coat—it coat 3 l —I could not have sold it for more than 1 l. 5 s.—I bought the shirts in Regent-street, and gave 9 s. a-piece for them—I could have got 8 s. a-piece if I had sold them—the prisoner was a tailor—he did not keep anything in that place—I had not known him before he came to lodge there—no other young men lodged in that room—I left him in bed at a quarter before five o'clock—he had no work to do, and I worked late and early—the landlady had called him that morning, and I got up.
ELLEN DAVIS . I am landlady of the house, I remember the prisoner and the prosecutor lodging with me—I went into the room at nine o'clock in the morning—no one bad been in between five and nine o'clock—I saw the shirt and coat which the prisoner left in the room, and missed the other things—the prisoner left me that day without notice, and owed me 8 s. 10 d.—he had not paid his lodging.
Cross-examined. Q. How long had he lodged with you? A. There months—he was out of work—a young man lodged in the next room—he he a brazier—he went out at six o'clock that morning—I did not see either of them go—nobody could go up to that room, because I sleep in the bottom of the house.
JAMES PAPER ( police-constable M 83.) I received information of this, but could not take the prisoner till lately.
WILLIAM SIMMONS ( police-constable L 150.) I produce the certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office ( read)—the prisoner is the man.
(Charles Hammond, of No.36, Pleasant-place, West-square, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY of stealing, value 4 l. 6 s. Aged 22.— Transported for Seven Years.
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Justice Park.
[trialAccount t18360229-872]
872. SAMUEL THOMAS was indicted for feloniously discharging at Richard Thomas, a certain gun, loaded with gunpowder and divers shots, with intent to kill and murder him.—2nd COUNT, stating his intent to be to do him some grievous bodily harm.
MESSRS. BODKIN, DOANE, and CHAMBERS, conducted the Prosecution.
RICHARD THOMAS . I live on Sydenham-hill, and am overseer of the poor of Dulwich parish, in Camberwell. I know the prisoner perfectly well—I have heard he has been in the police—he has not been chargeble on the parish since my time—he was employed on the parish roads to do work—I was on the committee of the highways—the sweepings of the highways were sold for the benefit of the parish it was the duty of the persons employed on the roads to put the sweepings in heaps, and leave it for the contractors, who come and take it away—I saw the prisoner to engaged on Saturday fortnight—I think that was the 13th of February—he was in company with a person named Dowse—I saw them wheeling the road-stuff off the road, towards the Greyhound—the prisoner had just returned—the other man had the barrow in his hand—seeing them so engaged, I first asked the prisoner where he was wheeling it—I understood him to say, "You may fined it out"—nothing more passed between me and the prisoner—I went a few yards distance, and thenasked Dowse where he was wheeling it—he pointed do the place where he had been—the prisoner might be near enough to hear that—Dowse did not say, to the Greyhound—he pointed to the place—I made a report of what I had seen to the Board—the result of that was, that the prisoner was dismissed by the chairman and the committee at large, (the committee of the highways)—on the following Monday—I left the vestry on the Wednesday evening, the 24th of February, on horseback—I went in the direction of my house—when I got past East Dulwich chapel, and the Plough public-house, I observed something on the road—it was half-past eight o'clock, and quite dark—immediately a voice called out to me, "Who are you?" or, "Who comes here"—I do not know which—I immediately drew up to the person addressing me, quite close—I could have touched him—I then saw distinctly who it was—it was the prisoner at the bar—I observed he had a gun in his hand—he said something, but I cannot recollect what—I was agitated and touched the more very quickly, and when I got a short distance I turned my head, and saw the prisoner levelling the gun, presenting a at me—I immediately threw myself on the neck of the mare,
and hung on—I got about the distance of the mare, and then fell on the road—I immediately scrambled and got up and ran off—I distinctly heard the snap of the gun—I am sure of it—I then ran on as fast as I could to the toll-gate—I was about 300 yards from it—the keeper's name is John Morris—I took shelter and remained there about twenty minutes—I saw the prisoner outside the gate while I was there—I heard him say to Morris he might as well be hung for a sheep as a lamb, and he was inviting me to come out—he was continually talking to Morris, but I did not hear what he said—he was full twenty minutes outside the toll-house—I had him taken into custody that night—I saw nothing more of the gun till it was produced at Union Hall—that was on Friday, I think—Simmonds, I belive, took him.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How long before this Wednesday night was it that you had made the report to the Board? A. On the Monday night—I met Mr. Hall on the Tuesday evening—I do not know how long it was before the dismissal was communicated to the prisoner—I saw him at work on Tuesday, in the middle of the day, as usual—I did not see him on Wednesday working on the road—I have not been to the Board since—I told the Board that Mr. Hall had given no orders for the road drift to be removed—I was about the length of the mare from him when he called, "Who goes there?"—it was dusk—I could not see distinctly who it was—when I fell on the ground the prisoner was about two or three lengths of the horse—I touched the mare with my heel—I do not wear spurs—I distinctly heard the snap—the mare's hoofs made no particular noise—not so much, I should think, as the click of a gun—the name of the place is Lordship-lane, Dulwich—I leaned forward and grasped the neck of the mare—I ran to the toll-house—I went home with a policeman—I did not see the prisoner again till he was at the office.
JOHN MORRIS . I am toll-keeper of the New-road gate, Dulwich. On the 24th February, in the evening, I saw Mr. Thomas about half-past eight o'clock—he was on foot, running—I pushed him into the toll-house—I saw the prisoner following him about twenty yards—there was light enough from the room to see him—I had known him before for years—I did not shut the door—Mr. Thomas slammed the door to to himself—the prisoner came up—I went through the gate and stopped and talked to him—the prisoner was walking, and had a gun in his hand—I walked out, and then he stopped still—he said he would shoot Mr. Thomas if he would come out—I said nothing to him about the gun—I was agaitated—the prisoner remained there from twenty minutes to half an hour, Mr. Thomas still continuing in the toll-house—the prisoner had the gun in his hand all the time—I did not notice the trigger—ha made no other expression, than that as Mr. Thomas had taken away his bread from him, he might as well be hung for a sheep as a lamb—he might as well be hung as starved to death—when the prisoner went, he went towards Sydenham—that is the direction for Mr. Thomas's house—they both live in the same direction—Mr. Thomas shortly after left—I went in, and told him the prisoner was gone—the prisoner came again in about half an hour—he knocked at the door, and asked if Mr. Thomas was there—I told him he was gone—he had the same gun—he was not in liquor, I am quite sure.
Cross-examined. Q. You say it was a moon-light night? A. Yes; I should think there was light enough for a person to see another at a mare's length—I could myself—I should think if he intended to shoot him, and not merely frighten him, he could have seen him sufficiently without calling
out to him at a mare's length—I did not say any thing to the Magistrates about the prisoner saying that Mr. Thomas had taken his bread from him, and he might as well be hanged as starved to death—they did not ask me so many question—the prisoner went away of his own accord.
CHARLES SIMMONNS ( police-constable P 153.) On the evening of Wednesday, the 24th, about a quarter before nine o'clock, I was going on duty from my own house—I met the prosecutor just as I got out of the door—in consequence of what he said, I took the prisoner into custody at the Crown public-house. in Dulwich—I had another policeman with me—I told him he must go along with me to the station-house—he told me he knew it; he expected it—there was a person in the room named John Junes, who said, "I did not think you meant it, thought you said it"—I had not at that time said what I took him for—on the road to the station. house he asked me what he was taken for—I said, "For attempting to shoot Mr. Thomas"—he told me he could not attempt to do that, as he had nothing but a piece of wood to do it with—I found no gun on him—when he got a little further, he laughed and said, although it was a gun, he had got nothing in it. On the next day I met John Jones—the prisoner was not with me. I got the gun I now have in my hand from Mrs. Dowse, who lives on Dulwich Common. I got it on Friday morning about ten o'clock—I examined the gun—it was not loaded—I examined the pan and touch-hole, and there was black powder—it presented the appearance of having been recently discharged.
Cross-examined. Q. A man of the name of Jones said he did not think he meant it, thought he said it? A. Yes—I got the gun from Mrs. Dowse on Friday, at ten in the morning.
ANN DOWSE . I am the wife of Thomas Dowse; the prisoner worked with my husband on the road, I remember Wednesday, the 24th—about five o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoner came and asked me if my husband was at home—I said no, he was gone to London—he said he wanted to borrow his gun—I lent him the gun—this is the same gun I lent—he went away—that was about five o'clock—I saw him again between nine and ten o'clock that evening—I heard the report of a gun just as I opened the door, and he said he had brought the gun—there was a knock at the door—the report was before the knock—I asked him if there was any danger—he said no, he had just let it off—I gave the gun to the policeman on Friday morning.
Cross-examined. Q. When was your husband discharged from working on the roads? A. On Wednesday morning he knew it—he came back about ten o'clock.
COURT. Q. Do you happen to know whether it was loaded when you gave it to the prisoner first? A. It was not loaded—we never keep it loaded—I have seven children.
ELIZABETH BEAMSLEY . I am a shop-keeper, and live at Dulwich; I knew the prisoner perfectly well for a great many years. On Wednesday ****evening, the 24th of last month, he came to me for an ounce of gunpowder and a quarter of a pound of shot—I served him—he paid me 21/2 d. the shot was No. 6—I counted it out into the scale, put it back into the bag, and gave it him with the bag.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you known him long? A. Yes—my house is a quarter of a mile from his.
WILLIAM EVAMY . I live at Norwood, and am an occasional waiter. I remember, on Wednesday evening, the 24th of February. I was at the tap-room
of the Crown Inn, Dulwich, between seven and eight o'clock—I saw several persons there, and among them the prisoner at the bar—when I went in, he was sitting down—he had a gun with him—I saw him get up, and take the gun with him to another part of the room, and do something with it—I saw him take something out of a bag with the bowl of a tobacco-pipe, and put it into the gun—he then put something into the pan of the gun; and when he had done, he sat down again—when he put something in the gun, I heard it make a grating noise, as I conceived, against the tobacco-pipe—I did not notice that it made any noise in the gun—some one said, "Are you going a-poaching?" or something like that—he said, "A man has shot a man, and I am going to shoot a man."
COURT. Q. Did you not say before the Magistrate that he said, "A man has shot me, and I am going to shoot a man?" A. That was my evidence before the Magistrate—after that he said Thomas had shot Thomas, and Thomas would shoot Thomas—I said, "I hope you are not going to commit any act to get yourself into any trouble."
Q. Did not you say "That is your joking way?" A. Yes, he is a jocular man at times—he made some short reply, that that was his business, or something—he went away in about a quarter of an hour, or twenty minutes—he said, "Good night, gentlemen"—he took the gun with him.
Cross-examined. Q. How many other persons were there in the room? A. There might be eight or ten—they were all about talking, not paying attention to him—I was a small distance from him—I was not paying particular attention—I was showing some books—I thought it was only his jokes.
WILLIAM OUZMAN . I am a journeyman butcher, living at Dulwich. I was in the tap of the Crown with the last witness, on Wednesday, the 24th—I was near the prisoner—I observed him put the ram-rod into the gun, and ram something down—I do not know what—he looked at the lock of the gun, lifted up the pan, and put it down again—I heard him say, when he sat down, that Mr. Thomas had shot him, and he intended shooting Mr. Thomas.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you ask him where Mr. Thomas wounded him? A. No—I knew Mr. Thomas—I was about a yard from the prisoner when he was ramming something down.
JOHN JONES . I am driver of a fly, and live at Dulwich, I was in the Crown tap on that evening—I saw the prisoner there—he was sitting with a gun—I saw him take something from a bag and put into the gun, which rattled down like shot, but I cannot say what it was—I heard him say Thomas had shot Thomas, and Thomas would shoot Thomas—he left between seven and eight o'clock—he came again about twenty-five minutes before eleven o'clock—I and another were drinking, and we asked him to drink with us—he asked if it was porter or half-and-half, and we said porter—he said he would drink with us, as, perhaps, it might be the last time—I saw Simmonds and Wilks come—Simmonds said, "I want you to go with me"—the prisoner said, "Very well," he was waiting for him, he expected him.
Cross-examined. Q. You drive a fly? A. Yes, in constant employment—I work for my brother, Jones, of Dulwich—I cannot say how long I had been in the public-house—I had been there about an hour before I saw the prisoner with his gun—I saw the two last witnesses there—I was about our yards from him when he put something, into the gun—there were
many persons taking in the house—there was a buz, but I heard the rattling in the gun—I saw no ramming, and nothing about the lock.
CHARLES COBDEN . I am one of the beadles of Camberwell parish. I knew the prisoner about three years and a half—he was all that time resident in the parish—he has lately been working on the roads—he received 14 s. a week—he was discharged on Tuesday evening, the 23rd—he told me so when I saw him next morning—he have his tools up to me—he walked with me round the end of the house to the gate, and then said, "Well, now I will go and get some powder and shot, and to a-shooting"—and I then said, "That will be but a very poor game, you will not kill enough to pay for powder and shot"—he said, "Oh, oh! I shall kill all that I want to kill"—and then he left me.
Cross-examined. Q. You are beadle of Camberwell parish? A. Yes; I do not know who contracts for the road-drift—I had no idea that Mr. Thomas was contractor at that time, but I have since heard different—I have since heard it was his man.
JOHN PETTY . I am a labouring man, in the employ of Mr. Thomas, the prosecutor. I remember on Wednesday, the 24th, being at my master's house—I saw the prisoner call there about a quarter before eight o'clock in the evening—he asked if my master was at home—I told him no—he asked me what time he would be at home—I asked the female-servant, and she told me, and I told him she expected he would be rather late—I asked if he had any message—he said no, he wanted to see him—I did not notice whether be ha any thing in his hand.
Cross-examined. Q. What servant are you? A. A day servant—I have nothing to do with the road-drift—one of his men has.
MR. PAYNE. to RICHARD THOMAS. Q. Where you on the Saturday fortnight by yourself, or by your servant, the contractor for the road drift? A. I was the sub-contractor—I was interested in it—I represented to the Board that the prisoner had been removing the drift contrary to the orders of Mr. Hall.
NOT GUILTY
Before Mr. Justice Park.
[trialAccount t18360229-873]
873. CHARLES CRANSTON, GEORGE CRANSTON, HENRY LAVELL , and JAMES FARRINGTON were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Ann Cranston, on the 27th of January, at St. Mary, Lambeth, Surrey, about the hour of seven in the night, with intent to steal, and feloniously and burglariously stealing therein 2 coats, value 4 l.; 2 pairs of trowsers, value 30 s.; 1 waistcoat, value 10 s.; 1 watch, value 2 l.; 1 watch-chain, value 5 s.; 1 watch-key, value 3 d.; 1 pair of braces, value 11 s.; 1 shirt, value 6 s.; 1 gown, value 10 s.; and 1 frock, value 3 s.; the goods of John Hines.
ANN HINES . I am going on for ten years of age—my father lived at No. 2, Commercial-buildings—we were living in Mr. Cranston's house, in the Commercial-buildings—my father and mother were lodgers there—Mrs. Cranston is the mother of two of the prisoners—we kept the parlours—we had only one room—the bed was there, as well as the table and chairs—my father and mother were not there at the time of the robbery—I was there by myself at half-past six o'clock—my father and mother went out at two o'clock—I went out for some coals that afternoon, about half-past five o'clock—I left nobody in the parlour—I locked the door, and took the key—I had to go as far as the Commercial-road—it was not quite dark—as
I was coming in with the coals, I saw George Cranston in the passage, at our parlour door, on the outside—I knew him very well—I could see him very well—it was not dark—he had a kettle in his hand, and asked me what sort of a fire I had—I told him it was almost out—I unlocked the door, and he rushed in without my will—he followed me in, and put his kettle on the fire—he did not stay many minutes—he went out of the room, and went up-stairs and brought down two pieces of bread—he then took away his kettle, and went up stairs again—his mother occupies the back-room up stairs—in our apartment there was a box belonging to my father, containing two coats, and two pairs of trowsers—it was locked, but I saw my mother put the things in—I remained in the room till a quarter past six o'clock—my father and mother had not come home then—I went into the yard, and locked the door—as I was locking it, I saw Charles Cranston standing in the passage—I took the key with me—I was not quite ten minutes out—when I came in again, before I unlocked the door, I head a rap at the window—I went to the street door—I then unlocked the parlour door, and saw George Cranston on the outside, putting the shutters too—I missed my father's box—the window-sash was up—it had been shut when I left the room—I saw my mother fasten it—I had not unfastened it—one pane of glass was broken—I had left two shillings on the mantel-piece, in a wine-glass—they were gone—I had known the prisoners before.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Are you father and mother here? A. Yes—no one told me to say that I saw George Cranston Putting the shutters too—I first told this story the second day I was at Queen-square—there was a week between the first and second day—I was frighted the first day he clerk to me—I did not talk with my father and mother of what I had seen—Mrs. Cranston said I was to come up-stairs, or my father would murder me—that was on the night the robbery was committed—I told that to my father—I said I would go and fetch my father after I had called her down—Mr. Cranston was not taken up—Mrs. Hawkins was not in the house that evening—she was out at work—her boy went and fetched her home—I did not take up a knife and say, I would kill myself if my father was sent for—I did not beg that my father might not be sent for, nor say any thing about his killing me—Cranston never boiled his kettle there before—I know they come home to ten every day—I was absent about ten minutes in the water-closet—I was not out playing that afternoon—I did not leave the key in the door, or the door open—I went out three times—I went with my father's ten about half-past four o'clock, and for some coals, and into the yard—I take my oath I went out where else.
COURT. Q. Was there any thing particular about George Cranston? A. Yes; he had a green shade over his eyes.
THOMAS DAVIES . I am a labourer. On the 27th of January, I was near No. 10, in the Commercial-buildings—I passed the prosecutor's house, and saw two men in the front yard—one had a shade over his eyes.
JASPER HAWKINS . I live in the same house with the Witness. As I was going out with my mother's candle, I saw Lavell waiting about the street—Farringdon was with him—they were walking backwards and forwards—I was not gone more than five minutes—they were there when I
came back—it was I told the girl of something I heard at the window—I saw nothing more.
Cross-examined. Q. You live in the same house, and that little girl played with you that afternoon? A. No; I only saw her go out at the back-yard—I hallooed out, "There is a noise at the window"—I saw her once before go to fill her kettle, after her parents went out—she had to go about twenty yards to get water.
HENRY TURNER . I know all the prisoners. On the 27th of January I was coming home from work, about twenty minutes past six o'clock—I passed the prosecutor's house—I saw Farringdon up against the wall, and Lavell stooping down, about ten feet from their yard they were in the street—I thought there was something up, and when I got to the top, I looked about half a minute, and then they were on the same spot—I then went home, and returned about half-past six o'clock—I saw Lavell pass with a box on his head, and three or four other men—he went straight down the street—one of the other men was Farringdon.
Cross-examined. Q. Lavell and Farringdon were near this yard? A. Yes, it is; Mr. Hines yard, he lodges there—I call it his yard—Farringdon lives about 200 feet off—Lavell had a frock coat and white trousers—Farringdon had white mole-skin clothes—I am sure of that—I cannot tell who the others were.
SAMUEL LOCK I produce the box—I found it within two doors of my house, No. 4, church-terrace, under the church wall—that is a quarter of a mile from the prosecutor's—it was in this broken state.
JOHN HINES This trunk is mine—it was not broken—when I left it, it was locked—I had in it at two o'clock, when I left the house, a watch two coals, two pairs of trowsers, and a waistcoat—the rest of the property belongs to my wife—I came home at eight o'clock—I had been home to dinner—the two Cranstons live in the same house—I work at Mr. Maltwood's factory at Lambeth.
Cross-examined. Q. Mrs. Cranston is the person to whom this house belongs? A. Yes—the prisoners lived with their mother—I was twice before the Magistrate—I have not spoken to my girl about what she should say. nor threatened her—I left the house in her care.
MARY SNELL I live in the same house with Mrs. Cranston—I was in her room when the sons came home to tea that night—they were in a hurry for it—George said to his mother, "I think I shall go down to Hines and boil my kettle"—Charles was in a hurry to go—George swore, and said, "What a hurry you are in."
Cross-examined. Q. Were you present when the little girl called coat to Mrs. Cranston, and the boy Hawkins? A. Yes—Mrs. Cranston went down stairs, and asked what was the matter—I followed her—Mrs. Cranston talked about going for her father—the girl was afraid for her father to be sent for, as she said be would murder her.
THOMAS WILLIAM BEDFORD ( police-sergeant L 17.) I took Farringdon into custody.
HENRY ROWS I am a foreman to Mr. Turner, of Waterloo-road. We had a watch offered as to pawn on the same evening, about seven o'clock, but did not take it in—we gave it back—two young men brought it, but I cannot swear to them.
Charles Cranston's Defence. I am innocent of the crime—I went home to tea and went not again and returned at twelve o'clock at night: and
the prosecutor said he thought I had robbed him—I said he had better give me in charge; he went for two policeman, and we were given in charge.
NOT GUILTY
Fifth Jury before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18360229-875]
875. ROBERT SMITH, JAMES HOWES , and JOHN ROOTS , were indicted for stealing, on the 29th of February, 300 lbs. weight of Potatoes, value 13 s.; and 3 sacks, value 3 s.; the goods of William Ellis, the master of Robert Smith.
CHARLES ELLIS I am the son of William Ellis, a potato-dealer, who lives in London-road. The prisoner Smith was in his service—last Monday morning at six o'clock I was in the kitchen—I looked through the window, and saw Smith in the yard with a lighted candle in his hand—he went into the potato warehouse, and returned without a light—he went out of a gate leading into York-street, and returned in about five minutes with a lighted candle—he then went to the bottom of the yard, where there is another gate leading into York-street—he then returned with a person who, from his dress and size, I thought to be Howes—they both went into the back warehouse; and the other man presently came out with what appeared to me to be a full sack of potatoes on his back—I could not see that man's face—Smith remained in the warehouse—I went up-stairs and told my father—I then came down, and distinctly saw Howes come out of the warehouse with a second sack—Smith then came out of the warehouse, and went about his ordinary business in the stable—I went out of the front door, and looked into York-street, but saw nothing there—I then walked to Moreton-street, where I saw a pony and cart—there was no one with it—I watched, and saw a man lead the pony and cart to our premises—it stopped at the back gate, and I heard something heavy put into the cart—the pony and cart then went on and stopped at the Rockingham Arms, at the corner of the Kent-road—the man went into the house—I went and looked at the cart, and saw the potatoes in it—the man then came out of the house—I saw it was the prisoner Roots—he went on with the cart—I followed him to Lock's-fields, Walworth, where I saw a policeman—I told him; and we followed the cart on to Clarence-buildings, where it stopped; and Roots took the potatoes into his own house—I went in and saw the potatoes in the two sacks, which had my father's name on them—we lost two sacks that morning—I came home and give information, and the other prisoners were taken.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS Q. Are you and your father in partnership? A. No—we employ three or four men—Smith, the carter, comes at five o'clock in the morning—the other men come about half-past six o'clock—I saw Smith go into the warehouse with a candle, and then he came out again—he came within a yard of me—he might have taken the candle out in his hand got a light at some shop—he then went to the bottom of the yard, and then returned and went into the warehouse with the other man—when I returned back from tracing the cart, Smith was gone out with his horse and cart.
RICHARD HAMBROOK I am a police-constable. I received information from the last witness, and followed the cart about half a mile—Roots drove it at contained two sacks of potatoes—they were taken out and put into Root's house—I took him just as he put the last sack down.
Smith's Defence. I went there at twenty minutes past five o'clock—I called my master and then lighted my candle at the Alfred's Head—I then
watered my horse, and it knocked down and candle—I tied the horse up, and went out and got a light again, and never saw any one on the premises.
Howes's Defence. I went home on Sunday night, and never left my bed till the officer took me on Monday morning.
CHARLES ELLIS re-examined. Q. You saw Smith go into the potato warehouse? A. Yes—he had no business there—he had been in our service three or four months—Howes had been in our service, but was discharged—I am positive it was him came in with Smith.
SMITH— GUILTY . Aged 20.
HOWES— GUILTY . Aged 35.
Transported for Seven Years.
ROOTS— NOT GUILTY
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18360229-875a]
875. JOHN DUDLEY was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. CHAMBERS conducted the prosecution.
EDWARD EASTWOOD ( police-constable R 39.) I was on duty in the Old Kent-road on the 30th of January, in the afternoon. I followed a woman about half a mile—she was then joined by another woman and the prisoner—I saw them conversing together—I went towards them—they observed me, and the two women got away—when I came to the prisoner, he had his hand in this left hand pocket—he drew his hand from his pocket—I seized it, and in it were these six counterfeit shillings—I took him to the station, and found on him four sixpences, one shilling, and 2 s. in copper, a penny doll, a caricature which I should judge cost a 1 d., a piece of netting, a lemon, and one reel of cotton, worth about 1 d.—he said he had found these shillings—he did not say where.
Prisoner. I saw six shillings, which I picked up, on the road, and they were full of dirt. Witness. They were enveloped in a ball of road dirt in his hand—it was between three and four o'clock.
JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of counterfeit coin. These are all counterfeit, and five of them are cast in the same mould—the dirt is used to make them appear as if dirty from circulation.
Prisoner. At the corner of the street I saw them lying on the dirt, and I took them up—I turned and did not see the policeman—I did not know they were bad.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined One Year.
876. JOHN DRUMMOND was attainted of burglary.
( See page 635.)
ADJOURNED TILL APRIL 4 TH.
